Yeah, hi. Um… what brought me in is, honestly, my head just won’t stay on one thing anymore. Like at work, I’m at the bench and I’ll be trying to focus on a piece, and then I’ll see a customer email pop up, and before I even finish reading that I’m noticing a saw mark I missed, and then there’s some noise outside or in the shop, and my attention just—just jumps to it.

And then I look back down and I’m like, wait, what was I doing? I’ll realize I’ve lost track of the whole work order, like what the customer actually wanted or what step I was on. It’s not just normal getting distracted, I don’t think. It’s been messing with my work because jewelry work is so detailed, you know, you can’t be halfway paying attention. I feel like my mind is constantly grabbing at every little thing instead of staying where I need it to be.

I’m not totally sure, honestly. I mean, I remember school being… school, you know? Like I wasn’t the kid who loved sitting still and doing worksheets forever, but I don’t know if it was the same kind of thing as what’s happening now.

I don’t really have a clear memory of teachers saying, like, “you can’t pay attention,” or anything like that. Maybe I zoned out sometimes, but I think a lot of kids do. I got through high school. I wasn’t, like, some perfect student, but I can’t say for sure this exact problem was there back then. It feels harder for me to pin down because when you’re a kid, you don’t really track your own attention in the same way. So… I guess my answer is I don’t know. Not that I can clearly remember.

not merely self-reproach or guilt about being sick).\n  manifestation: I keep replaying every client promise I messed up and telling myself I’m a fraud who embarrassed my family over one award.\n- key: S031\n  name: Diminished_Thinking\n  description: Diminished ability to think or concentrate, or indecisiveness.\n  manifestation: I’ll stare at a repair ticket or a tray of sapphires and can’t hold the measurements in my head long enough to decide what to do next.\n- key: S032\n  name: Death_Suicide\n  description: Recurrent thoughts of death, recurrent suicidal ideation, or a suicide attempt.\n  manifestation: I keep having thoughts that my family and my clients would be better off if I wasn’t around, and even though I don’t have a plan, it scares me how often it comes up.\n- key: S033\n  name: Elevated_Irritable_Mood\n  description: A distinct period of abnormally and persistently elevated, expansive, or irritable mood.\n  manifestation: After the show I felt lit up and larger than life one minute, then snapped at anyone who asked me to stick to normal shop hours or slow down.\n- key: S034\n  name: Increased_Activity\n  description: Abnormally and persistently increased goal-directed activity or energy.\n  manifestation: I couldn’t sit still at the bench; I’d bounce from sketching a new watch case to emailing suppliers to rearranging display trays long after closing.\n- key: S035\n  name: Inflated_Self_Esteem\n  description: Inflated self-esteem or grandiosity (may range from uncritical self-confidence to delusional proportions).\n  manifestation: Winning that ribbon made me feel like I’d finally proved I was on another level, like I could take every custom order and turn each one into a showpiece no one else around here could touch.\n- key: S036\n  name: Decreased_Sleep\n  description: Decreased need for sleep (e.g., feels rested after only 3 hours of sleep).\n  manifestation: For a while I was sleeping maybe two or three hours, then getting up clear-headed and excited to start cutting stones or posting new designs before sunrise.\n- key: S040\n  name: Increased_Goal_Activity\n  description: Increase in goal-directed activity (at work, school, socially, or sexually) or purposeless psychomotor agitation.\n  manifestation: I started lining up new commissions, messaging old customers, building a website plan, and sketching a whole Appalachian collection all at once, like I had to get it all moving right then.\n- key: functional_impairment\n  name: Functional Impairment\n  manifestation: I missed a major custom delivery and my shop records got so disorganized that I had to stop taking new work for now. My family has also been checking on me more because they got worried about how far I’d slipped from my usual routines.\n- key: duration\n  name: Duration\n  description: 2 weeks\n  manifestation: The wired, out-of-control stretch started right after the jewelry-show award and went on for about two weeks.\n- key: depressive_episode_duration\n  name: Depressive Episode Duration\n  description: 1 month\n  manifestation: The depressed, guilty feeling has been hanging over me for about a month now.\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Male / 27 / jeweler_or_precious_stone_or_metal_worker / high_school\n- chief_complaint_symptom_code: S039\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hi, it’s good to meet you. What brings you in today?\n\nPatient: Yeah, hi. Um… what brought me in is, honestly, my head just won’t stay on one thing anymore. Like at work, I’m at the bench and I’ll be trying to focus on a piece, and then I’ll see a customer email pop up, and before I even finish reading that I’m noticing a saw mark I missed, and then there’s some noise outside or in the shop, and my attention just—just jumps to it.\n\nAnd then I look back down and I’m like, wait, what was I doing? I’ll realize I’ve lost track of the whole work order, like what the customer actually wanted or what step I was on. It’s not just normal getting distracted, I don’t think. It’s been messing with my work because jewelry work is so detailed, you know, you can’t be halfway paying attention. I feel like my mind is constantly grabbing at every little thing instead of staying where I need it to be.\n\nDoctor: Did you have this same kind of trouble paying attention when you were a child?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure, honestly. I mean, I remember school being… school, you know? Like I wasn’t the kid who loved sitting still and doing worksheets forever, but I don’t know if it was the same kind of thing as what’s happening now.\n\nI don’t really have a clear memory of teachers saying, like, “you can’t pay attention,” or anything like that. Maybe I zoned out sometimes, but I think a lot of kids do. I got through high school. I wasn’t, like, some perfect student, but I can’t say for sure this exact problem was there back then. It feels harder for me to pin down because when you’re a kid, you don’t really track your own attention in the same way. So… I guess my answer is I don’t know. Not that I can clearly remember.\n\nDoctor: Did these concentration problems start after a particularly stressful or upsetting event in your life?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so. I mean, not that I can point to. There wasn’t, like, one big thing where before that I was fine and then after that my concentration fell apart.\n\nI’ve tried to think about that, because it would make more sense if I could say, “Oh, this started after this happened,” but I can’t really connect it to anything specific. Life has stress, work gets stressful, customers can be a lot, deadlines and repairs and all that, but I wouldn’t say there was some upsetting event that kicked it off. It feels more like it just became noticeable, or I started realizing how much it was getting in the way.\n\n[Last Question]\nDo you often find yourself worrying a lot, even when there isn’t one specific problem in front of you?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Um… I don’t know if I’d call it worrying a lot exactly. I mean, I think about things, sure—like work stuff or whether I’m forgetting something—but I don’t know that I’m sitting around anxious for no reason all the time.

It’s more like my mind keeps moving, if that makes sense. Sometimes I’ll be trying to do one thing and my thoughts are just kind of hopping around, and maybe some of those thoughts are worries, but I’m not sure it feels like worry as the main thing. I don’t wake up and think, like, “I’m worried and I don’t know why.” At least not that I’ve really noticed.

I guess I’d say… not especially? Or maybe I’m not sure what counts as “a lot.”

Not that I can clearly remember.\n\nDoctor: Did these concentration problems start after a particularly stressful or upsetting event in your life?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so. I mean, not that I can point to. There wasn’t, like, one big thing where before that I was fine and then after that my concentration fell apart.\n\nI’ve tried to think about that, because it would make more sense if I could say, “Oh, this started after this happened,” but I can’t really connect it to anything specific. Life has stress, work gets stressful, customers can be a lot, deadlines and repairs and all that, but I wouldn’t say there was some upsetting event that kicked it off. It feels more like it just became noticeable, or I started realizing how much it was getting in the way.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself worrying a lot, even when there isn’t one specific problem in front of you?\n\nPatient: Um… I don’t know if I’d call it worrying a lot exactly. I mean, I think about things, sure—like work stuff or whether I’m forgetting something—but I don’t know that I’m sitting around anxious for no reason all the time.\n\nIt’s more like my mind keeps moving, if that makes sense. Sometimes I’ll be trying to do one thing and my thoughts are just kind of hopping around, and maybe some of those thoughts are worries, but I’m not sure it feels like worry as the main thing. I don’t wake up and think, like, “I’m worried and I don’t know why.” At least not that I’ve really noticed.\n\nI guess I’d say… not especially? Or maybe I’m not sure what counts as “a lot.”\n\n[Last Question]\nHave you been having trouble sleeping?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. Do NOT force an unrelated item.\n- On your first reply of the consultation, always return the item whose key is your\n  chief_complaint_symptom_code, whatever the doctor opened with.\n- Do not answer the doctor, and do not write anything outside the JSON.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"key\": \"<key>\", \"name\": \"<name>\", \"description\": \"<description>\", \"reason\": \"<why this item>\"}\n"
  },

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah… I have, actually. Sleep has been kind of a mess lately.

Like, I’ll get into bed at what should be a normal time, because I’m trying to be responsible about it, and I’ll think, okay, I’m tired, I should just go to sleep. But then I’m just tossing around, flipping over, checking the time, getting annoyed that I’m still awake. It’s not even like I’m doing anything useful—I’m just lying there.

And then even if I do fall asleep, I’ve been waking up really early, before it’s even light out sometimes. My brain just kind of clicks on, and I’ll start thinking about that missed delivery at work, like replaying it or thinking about what I should’ve done, or what it’s going to mess up. And then once I’m up like that, it’s hard to really get back to sleep.

So by the time I actually have to get up, I don’t feel rested. I feel like I’m dragging myself out of bed, like I technically slept but it didn’t do what sleep is supposed to do.

Yeah… I have. I don’t really like saying that, but yes.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I’m not totally sure. I mean… maybe a little, but I don’t know how much is “a lot less than usual.”

Like with work, I still like the actual materials—the stones, the metal, getting something to sit just right, that tiny satisfying click when a setting works. That part of me is still there, I think. But I guess I haven’t been getting the same kind of lift from it lately. Sometimes I’m doing it and I know I should feel pleased, like, “That turned out nice,” but it just sort of lands flat.

But then I also don’t know if that counts as not enjoying things or if I’m just tired and in my head. I haven’t really been keeping track. It’s not like I woke up one day and hated everything I used to like. It’s more subtle than that, I guess. Things just feel… dulled down a bit.

My family has also been checking on me more because they got worried about how far I’d slipped from my usual routines.\n- key: duration\n  name: Duration\n  description: 2 weeks\n  manifestation: The wired, out-of-control stretch started right after the jewelry-show award and went on for about two weeks.\n- key: depressive_episode_duration\n  name: Depressive Episode Duration\n  description: 1 month\n  manifestation: The depressed, guilty feeling has been hanging over me for about a month now.\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Male / 27 / jeweler_or_precious_stone_or_metal_worker / high_school\n- chief_complaint_symptom_code: S039\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Have you been feeling down or depressed lately?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I have. I don’t really like saying that, but yes.\n\nThe last couple weeks especially, I’ll wake up and there’s just this heavy feeling already there, before anything has even happened. It’s hard to explain. It’s not like I wake up thinking, “Oh, this specific thing is terrible.” It’s more like there’s this hollow feeling in my chest, like everything has kind of lost its shine. Which sounds dramatic, especially because I work with things that are supposed to sparkle, you know? But that’s kind of how it feels.\n\nAnd I’ve had mornings where I get to the shop and I’m sitting in my truck before opening, and I just… I cry. Not for very long always, but enough that I have to pull myself together before I go in. I’ll sit there wiping my face and trying to look normal because I don’t want anyone asking what’s wrong. I don’t even always know what to say is wrong. It just feels like I’m carrying something really heavy around, and I’m tired of pretending I’m not.\n\nDoctor: Have you been enjoying things you usually like a lot less than usual?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure. I mean… maybe a little, but I don’t know how much is “a lot less than usual.”\n\nLike with work, I still like the actual materials—the stones, the metal, getting something to sit just right, that tiny satisfying click when a setting works. That part of me is still there, I think. But I guess I haven’t been getting the same kind of lift from it lately. Sometimes I’m doing it and I know I should feel pleased, like, “That turned out nice,” but it just sort of lands flat.\n\nBut then I also don’t know if that counts as not enjoying things or if I’m just tired and in my head. I haven’t really been keeping track. It’s not like I woke up one day and hated everything I used to like. It’s more subtle than that, I guess. Things just feel… dulled down a bit.\n\nDoctor: For at least the past two weeks, have you felt down or depressed most of the day nearly every day?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I mean, “most of the day” is hard because there are moments where I’m busy enough that I’m not actively thinking about it, especially if I’m focused on some tiny detail at the bench. But underneath it, it’s been there pretty much every day these past couple weeks.\n\nI wake up with it already sitting on me, like this heavy, hollow feeling, before anything has even happened. It’s like the shine has gone out of everything a little. And I’ve had mornings where I’m in my truck before opening the shop and I just start crying, and then I have to sit there and get myself together so I can go in and act normal. So… yeah, I’d say it’s been most days, and a lot of the day.\n\n[Last Question]\nHave you noticed your appetite being lower than usual lately?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I don’t really know. I haven’t been paying close attention to it, honestly.

Some days I eat because it’s just time to eat, not because I’m excited about food, but I’m not sure if that means my appetite is actually lower or if I’m just distracted and kind of going through the motions. Like, I’ll realize I haven’t thought about lunch, but then if food is there I can usually eat it. So I wouldn’t say I’ve clearly noticed a big drop or anything.

It’s more that meals feel like one more thing I’m supposed to do, if that makes sense. But no, I don’t have a definite answer like, “Yes, I’m barely eating.” I’m not sure.

Like at work, I’m at the bench and I’ll be trying to focus on a piece, and then I’ll see a customer email pop up, and before I even finish reading that I’m noticing a saw mark I missed, and then there’s some noise outside or in the shop, and my attention just—just jumps to it.\n\nAnd then I look back down and I’m like, wait, what was I doing? I’ll realize I’ve lost track of the whole work order, like what the customer actually wanted or what step I was on. It’s not just normal getting distracted, I don’t think. It’s been messing with my work because jewelry work is so detailed, you know, you can’t be halfway paying attention. I feel like my mind is constantly grabbing at every little thing instead of staying where I need it to be.\n\nDoctor: Did you have this same kind of trouble paying attention when you were a child?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure, honestly. I mean, I remember school being… school, you know? Like I wasn’t the kid who loved sitting still and doing worksheets forever, but I don’t know if it was the same kind of thing as what’s happening now.\n\nI don’t really have a clear memory of teachers saying, like, “you can’t pay attention,” or anything like that. Maybe I zoned out sometimes, but I think a lot of kids do. I got through high school. I wasn’t, like, some perfect student, but I can’t say for sure this exact problem was there back then. It feels harder for me to pin down because when you’re a kid, you don’t really track your own attention in the same way. So… I guess my answer is I don’t know. Not that I can clearly remember.\n\nDoctor: Did these concentration problems start after a particularly stressful or upsetting event in your life?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so. I mean, not that I can point to. There wasn’t, like, one big thing where before that I was fine and then after that my concentration fell apart.\n\nI’ve tried to think about that, because it would make more sense if I could say, “Oh, this started after this happened,” but I can’t really connect it to anything specific. Life has stress, work gets stressful, customers can be a lot, deadlines and repairs and all that, but I wouldn’t say there was some upsetting event that kicked it off. It feels more like it just became noticeable, or I started realizing how much it was getting in the way.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself worrying a lot, even when there isn’t one specific problem in front of you?\n\nPatient: Um… I don’t know if I’d call it worrying a lot exactly. I mean, I think about things, sure—like work stuff or whether I’m forgetting something—but I don’t know that I’m sitting around anxious for no reason all the time.\n\nIt’s more like my mind keeps moving, if that makes sense. Sometimes I’ll be trying to do one thing and my thoughts are just kind of hopping around, and maybe some of those thoughts are worries, but I’m not sure it feels like worry as the main thing. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah. Definitely. That’s actually been one of the more obvious things, I think.

It’s not just normal tired, like “I had a long day at work.” It’s more like my body doesn’t want to cooperate. When I’m sitting at the bench, my arms feel like they’re made of lead sometimes. Even doing something I’ve done a thousand times—polishing a setting, holding steady for detail work, moving trays around—it feels like it takes way more out of me than it should.

And carrying a tray of stones or getting things set up, stuff that normally wouldn’t even register as effort, can wipe me out. I’ll think, “Why am I this tired from that?” It’s embarrassing, honestly, because my work isn’t easy, but I know what normal work-tired feels like, and this feels different.

Even if I get a full night off, it doesn’t really reset me. I can rest, or not do much, and then I’m still dragging the next day. It’s like the tiredness is already there waiting for me before I even start.

I mean, not that I can point to. There wasn’t, like, one big thing where before that I was fine and then after that my concentration fell apart.\n\nI’ve tried to think about that, because it would make more sense if I could say, “Oh, this started after this happened,” but I can’t really connect it to anything specific. Life has stress, work gets stressful, customers can be a lot, deadlines and repairs and all that, but I wouldn’t say there was some upsetting event that kicked it off. It feels more like it just became noticeable, or I started realizing how much it was getting in the way.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself worrying a lot, even when there isn’t one specific problem in front of you?\n\nPatient: Um… I don’t know if I’d call it worrying a lot exactly. I mean, I think about things, sure—like work stuff or whether I’m forgetting something—but I don’t know that I’m sitting around anxious for no reason all the time.\n\nIt’s more like my mind keeps moving, if that makes sense. Sometimes I’ll be trying to do one thing and my thoughts are just kind of hopping around, and maybe some of those thoughts are worries, but I’m not sure it feels like worry as the main thing. I don’t wake up and think, like, “I’m worried and I don’t know why.” At least not that I’ve really noticed.\n\nI guess I’d say… not especially? Or maybe I’m not sure what counts as “a lot.”\n\nDoctor: Have you been having trouble sleeping?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I have, actually. Sleep has been kind of a mess lately.\n\nLike, I’ll get into bed at what should be a normal time, because I’m trying to be responsible about it, and I’ll think, okay, I’m tired, I should just go to sleep. But then I’m just tossing around, flipping over, checking the time, getting annoyed that I’m still awake. It’s not even like I’m doing anything useful—I’m just lying there.\n\nAnd then even if I do fall asleep, I’ve been waking up really early, before it’s even light out sometimes. My brain just kind of clicks on, and I’ll start thinking about that missed delivery at work, like replaying it or thinking about what I should’ve done, or what it’s going to mess up. And then once I’m up like that, it’s hard to really get back to sleep.\n\nSo by the time I actually have to get up, I don’t feel rested. I feel like I’m dragging myself out of bed, like I technically slept but it didn’t do what sleep is supposed to do.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling down or depressed lately?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I have. I don’t really like saying that, but yes.\n\nThe last couple weeks especially, I’ll wake up and there’s just this heavy feeling already there, before anything has even happened. It’s hard to explain. It’s not like I wake up thinking, “Oh, this specific thing is terrible.” It’s more like there’s this hollow feeling in my chest, like everything has kind of lost its shine. Which sounds dramatic, especially because I work with things that are supposed to sparkle, you know? But that’s kind of how it feels.\n\nAnd I’ve had mornings where I get to the shop and I’m sitting in my truck before opening, and I just… I cry. Not for very long always, but enough that I have to pull myself together before I go in. I’ll sit there wiping my face and trying to look normal because I don’t want anyone asking what’s wrong. I don’t even always know what to say is wrong. It just feels like I’m carrying something really heavy around, and I’m tired of pretending I’m not.\n\nDoctor: Have you been enjoying things you usually like a lot less than usual?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure. I mean… maybe a little, but I don’t know how much is “a lot less than usual.”\n\nLike with work, I still like the actual materials—the stones, the metal, getting something to sit just right, that tiny satisfying click when a setting works. That part of me is still there, I think. But I guess I haven’t been getting the same kind of lift from it lately. Sometimes I’m doing it and I know I should feel pleased, like, “That turned out nice,” but it just sort of lands flat.\n\nBut then I also don’t know if that counts as not enjoying things or if I’m just tired and in my head. I haven’t really been keeping track. It’s not like I woke up one day and hated everything I used to like. It’s more subtle than that, I guess. Things just feel… dulled down a bit.\n\nDoctor: For at least the past two weeks, have you felt down or depressed most of the day nearly every day?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I mean, “most of the day” is hard because there are moments where I’m busy enough that I’m not actively thinking about it, especially if I’m focused on some tiny detail at the bench. But underneath it, it’s been there pretty much every day these past couple weeks.\n\nI wake up with it already sitting on me, like this heavy, hollow feeling, before anything has even happened. It’s like the shine has gone out of everything a little. And I’ve had mornings where I’m in my truck before opening the shop and I just start crying, and then I have to sit there and get myself together so I can go in and act normal. So… yeah, I’d say it’s been most days, and a lot of the day.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed your appetite being lower than usual lately?\n\nPatient: I don’t really know. I haven’t been paying close attention to it, honestly.\n\nSome days I eat because it’s just time to eat, not because I’m excited about food, but I’m not sure if that means my appetite is actually lower or if I’m just distracted and kind of going through the motions. Like, I’ll realize I haven’t thought about lunch, but then if food is there I can usually eat it. So I wouldn’t say I’ve clearly noticed a big drop or anything.\n\nIt’s more that meals feel like one more thing I’m supposed to do, if that makes sense. But no, I don’t have a definite answer like, “Yes, I’m barely eating.” I’m not sure.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling unusually tired or low on energy lately?\n\nPatient: Yeah. Definitely. That’s actually been one of the more obvious things, I think.\n\nIt’s not just normal tired, like “I had a long day at work.” It’s more like my body doesn’t want to cooperate. When I’m sitting at the bench, my arms feel like they’re made of lead sometimes. Even doing something I’ve done a thousand times—polishing a setting, holding steady for detail work, moving trays around—it feels like it takes way more out of me than it should.\n\nAnd carrying a tray of stones or getting things set up, stuff that normally wouldn’t even register as effort, can wipe me out. I’ll think, “Why am I this tired from that?” It’s embarrassing, honestly, because my work isn’t easy, but I know what normal work-tired feels like, and this feels different.\n\nEven if I get a full night off, it doesn’t really reset me. I can rest, or not do much, and then I’m still dragging the next day. It’s like the tiredness is already there waiting for me before I even start.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Yeah… yeah, I have. Probably more than I want to admit.

It’s like my brain keeps pulling up every client I promised something to and then didn’t follow through the way I should’ve. Even if it was fixable, even if nobody yelled at me, I keep replaying it like, “You told them you could handle this, and then you made yourself look stupid.” Or I’ll think about little mistakes at the shop and it turns into this whole thing where I’m not just a person who made a mistake, I’m a fraud. Like I’ve been pretending I’m better at this than I really am.

And the family part… that’s been eating at me too. I had this award, and everyone made such a big deal out of it, like it meant I was really going somewhere. And now I feel like I embarrassed them by not living up to it. Like they were proud over this one thing, and I let them believe it meant something bigger than it did.

I know that probably sounds dramatic when I say it out loud, but it feels very real in my head. I’ll sit there and go over it again and again, and it’s hard to talk myself out of it.

I don’t even always know what to say is wrong. I mean… maybe a little, but I don’t know how much is “a lot less than usual.”\n\nLike with work, I still like the actual materials—the stones, the metal, getting something to sit just right, that tiny satisfying click when a setting works. That part of me is still there, I think. But I guess I haven’t been getting the same kind of lift from it lately. Sometimes I’m doing it and I know I should feel pleased, like, “That turned out nice,” but it just sort of lands flat.\n\nBut then I also don’t know if that counts as not enjoying things or if I’m just tired and in my head. I haven’t really been keeping track. It’s not like I woke up one day and hated everything I used to like. It’s more subtle than that, I guess. Things just feel… dulled down a bit.\n\nDoctor: For at least the past two weeks, have you felt down or depressed most of the day nearly every day?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I mean, “most of the day” is hard because there are moments where I’m busy enough that I’m not actively thinking about it, especially if I’m focused on some tiny detail at the bench. But underneath it, it’s been there pretty much every day these past couple weeks.\n\nI wake up with it already sitting on me, like this heavy, hollow feeling, before anything has even happened. It’s like the shine has gone out of everything a little. And I’ve had mornings where I’m in my truck before opening the shop and I just start crying, and then I have to sit there and get myself together so I can go in and act normal. So… yeah, I’d say it’s been most days, and a lot of the day.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed your appetite being lower than usual lately?\n\nPatient: I don’t really know. I haven’t been paying close attention to it, honestly.\n\nSome days I eat because it’s just time to eat, not because I’m excited about food, but I’m not sure if that means my appetite is actually lower or if I’m just distracted and kind of going through the motions. Like, I’ll realize I haven’t thought about lunch, but then if food is there I can usually eat it. So I wouldn’t say I’ve clearly noticed a big drop or anything.\n\nIt’s more that meals feel like one more thing I’m supposed to do, if that makes sense. But no, I don’t have a definite answer like, “Yes, I’m barely eating.” I’m not sure.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling unusually tired or low on energy lately?\n\nPatient: Yeah. Definitely. That’s actually been one of the more obvious things, I think.\n\nIt’s not just normal tired, like “I had a long day at work.” It’s more like my body doesn’t want to cooperate. When I’m sitting at the bench, my arms feel like they’re made of lead sometimes. Even doing something I’ve done a thousand times—polishing a setting, holding steady for detail work, moving trays around—it feels like it takes way more out of me than it should.\n\nAnd carrying a tray of stones or getting things set up, stuff that normally wouldn’t even register as effort, can wipe me out. I’ll think, “Why am I this tired from that?” It’s embarrassing, honestly, because my work isn’t easy, but I know what normal work-tired feels like, and this feels different.\n\nEven if I get a full night off, it doesn’t really reset me. I can rest, or not do much, and then I’m still dragging the next day. It’s like the tiredness is already there waiting for me before I even start.\n\nDoctor: Lately, have you been feeling worthless or blaming yourself a lot?\n\nPatient: Yeah… yeah, I have. Probably more than I want to admit.\n\nIt’s like my brain keeps pulling up every client I promised something to and then didn’t follow through the way I should’ve. Even if it was fixable, even if nobody yelled at me, I keep replaying it like, “You told them you could handle this, and then you made yourself look stupid.” Or I’ll think about little mistakes at the shop and it turns into this whole thing where I’m not just a person who made a mistake, I’m a fraud. Like I’ve been pretending I’m better at this than I really am.\n\nAnd the family part… that’s been eating at me too. I had this award, and everyone made such a big deal out of it, like it meant I was really going somewhere. And now I feel like I embarrassed them by not living up to it. Like they were proud over this one thing, and I let them believe it meant something bigger than it did.\n\nI know that probably sounds dramatic when I say it out loud, but it feels very real in my head. I’ll sit there and go over it again and again, and it’s hard to talk myself out of it.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. I’ll sit there and go over it again and again, and it’s hard to talk myself out of it.\n\n[Last Question]\nHave you had thoughts that you would be better off dead, or thoughts of hurting yourself?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Male / 27 / jeweler_or_precious_stone_or_metal_worker / high_school\n- severity: Moderate\n\n[Targeted Info]\nname: Death_Suicide\nmanifestation: \"I keep having thoughts that my family and my clients would be better off if I wasn’t around, and even though I don’t have a plan, it scares me how often it comes up.\"\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Verbose patient.\nA verbose patient may\n1) provide excessively detailed responses when describing their symptoms and experiences, even when the details are directly relevant,\n2) elaborate extensively on the background, events, thoughts, feelings, and personal experiences surrounding their symptoms, and\n3) demonstrate difficulty allowing the doctor to guide the interview or move on to another clinical topic.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah… I have. I don’t really like saying that out loud, but yes.

It’s not like I have some plan or that I’ve sat down and decided I’m going to do something to myself. I haven’t. But the thought keeps coming up that maybe people would be better off if I just wasn’t around. Like my family wouldn’t have to keep being disappointed or worried, and my clients wouldn’t be relying on me and getting let down. It’s this awful loop where I think, “Everyone’s life would probably be easier without me in it.”

And then I get scared because it’s not just a random thought once and then it goes away. It comes back more often than I’m comfortable with. I’ll be trying to do something normal, or I’ll be alone for a minute, and there it is again. I don’t want to feel that way, and part of me knows it’s probably not a fair way to look at things, but in the moment it feels really convincing.

So… yes, I’ve had thoughts like that. Not a plan, but the thoughts are there, and they scare me.

From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n12\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF32.A, F90.0, F41.9, F51.9\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hi, it’s good to meet you. What brings you in today?\n\nPatient: Yeah, hi. Um… what brought me in is, honestly, my head just won’t stay on one thing anymore. Like at work, I’m at the bench and I’ll be trying to focus on a piece, and then I’ll see a customer email pop up, and before I even finish reading that I’m noticing a saw mark I missed, and then there’s some noise outside or in the shop, and my attention just—just jumps to it.\n\nAnd then I look back down and I’m like, wait, what was I doing? I’ll realize I’ve lost track of the whole work order, like what the customer actually wanted or what step I was on. It’s not just normal getting distracted, I don’t think. It’s been messing with my work because jewelry work is so detailed, you know, you can’t be halfway paying attention. I feel like my mind is constantly grabbing at every little thing instead of staying where I need it to be.\n\nDoctor: Did you have this same kind of trouble paying attention when you were a child?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure, honestly. I mean, I remember school being… school, you know? Like I wasn’t the kid who loved sitting still and doing worksheets forever, but I don’t know if it was the same kind of thing as what’s happening now.\n\nI don’t really have a clear memory of teachers saying, like, “you can’t pay attention,” or anything like that. Maybe I zoned out sometimes, but I think a lot of kids do. I got through high school. I wasn’t, like, some perfect student, but I can’t say for sure this exact problem was there back then. It feels harder for me to pin down because when you’re a kid, you don’t really track your own attention in the same way. So… I guess my answer is I don’t know. Not that I can clearly remember.\n\nDoctor: Did these concentration problems start after a particularly stressful or upsetting event in your life?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so. I mean, not that I can point to. There wasn’t, like, one big thing where before that I was fine and then after that my concentration fell apart.\n\nI’ve tried to think about that, because it would make more sense if I could say, “Oh, this started after this happened,” but I can’t really connect it to anything specific. Life has stress, work gets stressful, customers can be a lot, deadlines and repairs and all that, but I wouldn’t say there was some upsetting event that kicked it off. It feels more like it just became noticeable, or I started realizing how much it was getting in the way.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself worrying a lot, even when there isn’t one specific problem in front of you?\n\nPatient: Um… I don’t know if I’d call it worrying a lot exactly. I mean, I think about things, sure—like work stuff or whether I’m forgetting something—but I don’t know that I’m sitting around anxious for no reason all the time.\n\nIt’s more like my mind keeps moving, if that makes sense. Sometimes I’ll be trying to do one thing and my thoughts are just kind of hopping around, and maybe some of those thoughts are worries, but I’m not sure it feels like worry as the main thing. I don’t wake up and think, like, “I’m worried and I don’t know why.” At least not that I’ve really noticed.\n\nI guess I’d say… not especially? Or maybe I’m not sure what counts as “a lot.”\n\nDoctor: Have you been having trouble sleeping?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I have, actually. Sleep has been kind of a mess lately.\n\nLike, I’ll get into bed at what should be a normal time, because I’m trying to be responsible about it, and I’ll think, okay, I’m tired, I should just go to sleep. But then I’m just tossing around, flipping over, checking the time, getting annoyed that I’m still awake. It’s not even like I’m doing anything useful—I’m just lying there.\n\nAnd then even if I do fall asleep, I’ve been waking up really early, before it’s even light out sometimes. My brain just kind of clicks on, and I’ll start thinking about that missed delivery at work, like replaying it or thinking about what I should’ve done, or what it’s going to mess up. And then once I’m up like that, it’s hard to really get back to sleep.\n\nSo by the time I actually have to get up, I don’t feel rested. I feel like I’m dragging myself out of bed, like I technically slept but it didn’t do what sleep is supposed to do.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling down or depressed lately?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I have. I don’t really like saying that, but yes.\n\nThe last couple weeks especially, I’ll wake up and there’s just this heavy feeling already there, before anything has even happened. It’s hard to explain. It’s not like I wake up thinking, “Oh, this specific thing is terrible.” It’s more like there’s this hollow feeling in my chest, like everything has kind of lost its shine. Which sounds dramatic, especially because I work with things that are supposed to sparkle, you know? But that’s kind of how it feels.\n\nAnd I’ve had mornings where I get to the shop and I’m sitting in my truck before opening, and I just… I cry. Not for very long always, but enough that I have to pull myself together before I go in. I’ll sit there wiping my face and trying to look normal because I don’t want anyone asking what’s wrong. I don’t even always know what to say is wrong. It just feels like I’m carrying something really heavy around, and I’m tired of pretending I’m not.\n\nDoctor: Have you been enjoying things you usually like a lot less than usual?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure. I mean… maybe a little, but I don’t know how much is “a lot less than usual.”\n\nLike with work, I still like the actual materials—the stones, the metal, getting something to sit just right, that tiny satisfying click when a setting works. That part of me is still there, I think. But I guess I haven’t been getting the same kind of lift from it lately. Sometimes I’m doing it and I know I should feel pleased, like, “That turned out nice,” but it just sort of lands flat.\n\nBut then I also don’t know if that counts as not enjoying things or if I’m just tired and in my head. I haven’t really been keeping track. It’s not like I woke up one day and hated everything I used to like. It’s more subtle than that, I guess. Things just feel… dulled down a bit.\n\nDoctor: For at least the past two weeks, have you felt down or depressed most of the day nearly every day?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I mean, “most of the day” is hard because there are moments where I’m busy enough that I’m not actively thinking about it, especially if I’m focused on some tiny detail at the bench. But underneath it, it’s been there pretty much every day these past couple weeks.\n\nI wake up with it already sitting on me, like this heavy, hollow feeling, before anything has even happened. It’s like the shine has gone out of everything a little. And I’ve had mornings where I’m in my truck before opening the shop and I just start crying, and then I have to sit there and get myself together so I can go in and act normal. So… yeah, I’d say it’s been most days, and a lot of the day.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed your appetite being lower than usual lately?\n\nPatient: I don’t really know. I haven’t been paying close attention to it, honestly.\n\nSome days I eat because it’s just time to eat, not because I’m excited about food, but I’m not sure if that means my appetite is actually lower or if I’m just distracted and kind of going through the motions. Like, I’ll realize I haven’t thought about lunch, but then if food is there I can usually eat it. So I wouldn’t say I’ve clearly noticed a big drop or anything.\n\nIt’s more that meals feel like one more thing I’m supposed to do, if that makes sense. But no, I don’t have a definite answer like, “Yes, I’m barely eating.” I’m not sure.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling unusually tired or low on energy lately?\n\nPatient: Yeah. Definitely. That’s actually been one of the more obvious things, I think.\n\nIt’s not just normal tired, like “I had a long day at work.” It’s more like my body doesn’t want to cooperate. When I’m sitting at the bench, my arms feel like they’re made of lead sometimes. Even doing something I’ve done a thousand times—polishing a setting, holding steady for detail work, moving trays around—it feels like it takes way more out of me than it should.\n\nAnd carrying a tray of stones or getting things set up, stuff that normally wouldn’t even register as effort, can wipe me out. I’ll think, “Why am I this tired from that?” It’s embarrassing, honestly, because my work isn’t easy, but I know what normal work-tired feels like, and this feels different.\n\nEven if I get a full night off, it doesn’t really reset me. Like they were proud over this one thing, and I let them believe it meant something bigger than it did.\n\nI know that probably sounds dramatic when I say it out loud, but it feels very real in my head. I’m not really sure, honestly. I haven’t noticed myself moving or talking slower in a way where I could clearly say, “Yes, that’s happening.”

I mean, I’ve felt slowed down inside sometimes, if that makes sense, but I don’t know if other people would actually see it. No one’s said, like, “Why are you talking so slow?” or anything like that. I feel like I’m dragging myself out of bed, like I technically slept but it didn’t do what sleep is supposed to do.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling down or depressed lately?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I have. I don’t really like saying that, but yes.\n\nThe last couple weeks especially, I’ll wake up and there’s just this heavy feeling already there, before anything has even happened. It’s hard to explain. It’s not like I wake up thinking, “Oh, this specific thing is terrible.” It’s more like there’s this hollow feeling in my chest, like everything has kind of lost its shine. Which sounds dramatic, especially because I work with things that are supposed to sparkle, you know? But that’s kind of how it feels.\n\nAnd I’ve had mornings where I get to the shop and I’m sitting in my truck before opening, and I just… I cry. Not for very long always, but enough that I have to pull myself together before I go in. I’ll sit there wiping my face and trying to look normal because I don’t want anyone asking what’s wrong. I don’t even always know what to say is wrong. It just feels like I’m carrying something really heavy around, and I’m tired of pretending I’m not.\n\nDoctor: Have you been enjoying things you usually like a lot less than usual?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure. I mean… maybe a little, but I don’t know how much is “a lot less than usual.”\n\nLike with work, I still like the actual materials—the stones, the metal, getting something to sit just right, that tiny satisfying click when a setting works. That part of me is still there, I think. But I guess I haven’t been getting the same kind of lift from it lately. Sometimes I’m doing it and I know I should feel pleased, like, “That turned out nice,” but it just sort of lands flat.\n\nBut then I also don’t know if that counts as not enjoying things or if I’m just tired and in my head. I haven’t really been keeping track. It’s not like I woke up one day and hated everything I used to like. It’s more subtle than that, I guess. I haven’t been paying close attention to it, honestly.\n\nSome days I eat because it’s just time to eat, not because I’m excited about food, but I’m not sure if that means my appetite is actually lower or if I’m just distracted and kind of going through the motions. Like, I’ll realize I haven’t thought about lunch, but then if food is there I can usually eat it. When I’m sitting at the bench, my arms feel like they’re made of lead sometimes. I can rest, or not do much, and then I’m still dragging the next day. Probably more than I want to admit.\n\nIt’s like my brain keeps pulling up every client I promised something to and then didn’t follow through the way I should’ve. Like they were proud over this one thing, and I let them believe it meant something bigger than it did.\n\nI know that probably sounds dramatic when I say it out loud, but it feels very real in my head. I’m honestly not sure how to answer that one cleanly.

I mean, I know what you’re asking, like whether stuff I normally enjoy feels flat or pointless, but I haven’t really been keeping track of it in that way. If you asked me to list what I usually enjoy and then compare it to the last two weeks, I’d probably have to sit and think about each thing separately.

So… I guess I’d say I’m not totally sure. I haven’t clearly noticed a major drop, but I also don’t feel like I can give you a confident yes or no.

    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n14\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF32.2, F90.0, F41.9, F51.9\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hi, it’s good to meet you. What brings you in today?\n\nPatient: Yeah, hi. Um… what brought me in is, honestly, my head just won’t stay on one thing anymore. Like at work, I’m at the bench and I’ll be trying to focus on a piece, and then I’ll see a customer email pop up, and before I even finish reading that I’m noticing a saw mark I missed, and then there’s some noise outside or in the shop, and my attention just—just jumps to it.\n\nAnd then I look back down and I’m like, wait, what was I doing? I’ll realize I’ve lost track of the whole work order, like what the customer actually wanted or what step I was on. It’s not just normal getting distracted, I don’t think. It’s been messing with my work because jewelry work is so detailed, you know, you can’t be halfway paying attention. I feel like my mind is constantly grabbing at every little thing instead of staying where I need it to be.\n\nDoctor: Did you have this same kind of trouble paying attention when you were a child?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure, honestly. I mean, I remember school being… school, you know? Like I wasn’t the kid who loved sitting still and doing worksheets forever, but I don’t know if it was the same kind of thing as what’s happening now.\n\nI don’t really have a clear memory of teachers saying, like, “you can’t pay attention,” or anything like that. Maybe I zoned out sometimes, but I think a lot of kids do. I got through high school. I wasn’t, like, some perfect student, but I can’t say for sure this exact problem was there back then. It feels harder for me to pin down because when you’re a kid, you don’t really track your own attention in the same way. So… I guess my answer is I don’t know. Not that I can clearly remember.\n\nDoctor: Did these concentration problems start after a particularly stressful or upsetting event in your life?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so. I mean, not that I can point to. There wasn’t, like, one big thing where before that I was fine and then after that my concentration fell apart.\n\nI’ve tried to think about that, because it would make more sense if I could say, “Oh, this started after this happened,” but I can’t really connect it to anything specific. Life has stress, work gets stressful, customers can be a lot, deadlines and repairs and all that, but I wouldn’t say there was some upsetting event that kicked it off. It feels more like it just became noticeable, or I started realizing how much it was getting in the way.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself worrying a lot, even when there isn’t one specific problem in front of you?\n\nPatient: Um… I don’t know if I’d call it worrying a lot exactly. I mean, I think about things, sure—like work stuff or whether I’m forgetting something—but I don’t know that I’m sitting around anxious for no reason all the time.\n\nIt’s more like my mind keeps moving, if that makes sense. Sometimes I’ll be trying to do one thing and my thoughts are just kind of hopping around, and maybe some of those thoughts are worries, but I’m not sure it feels like worry as the main thing. I don’t wake up and think, like, “I’m worried and I don’t know why.” At least not that I’ve really noticed.\n\nI guess I’d say… not especially? Or maybe I’m not sure what counts as “a lot.”\n\nDoctor: Have you been having trouble sleeping?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I have, actually. Sleep has been kind of a mess lately.\n\nLike, I’ll get into bed at what should be a normal time, because I’m trying to be responsible about it, and I’ll think, okay, I’m tired, I should just go to sleep. But then I’m just tossing around, flipping over, checking the time, getting annoyed that I’m still awake. It’s not even like I’m doing anything useful—I’m just lying there.\n\nAnd then even if I do fall asleep, I’ve been waking up really early, before it’s even light out sometimes. My brain just kind of clicks on, and I’ll start thinking about that missed delivery at work, like replaying it or thinking about what I should’ve done, or what it’s going to mess up. And then once I’m up like that, it’s hard to really get back to sleep.\n\nSo by the time I actually have to get up, I don’t feel rested. I feel like I’m dragging myself out of bed, like I technically slept but it didn’t do what sleep is supposed to do.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling down or depressed lately?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I have. I don’t really like saying that, but yes.\n\nThe last couple weeks especially, I’ll wake up and there’s just this heavy feeling already there, before anything has even happened. It’s hard to explain. It’s not like I wake up thinking, “Oh, this specific thing is terrible.” It’s more like there’s this hollow feeling in my chest, like everything has kind of lost its shine. Which sounds dramatic, especially because I work with things that are supposed to sparkle, you know? But that’s kind of how it feels.\n\nAnd I’ve had mornings where I get to the shop and I’m sitting in my truck before opening, and I just… I cry. Not for very long always, but enough that I have to pull myself together before I go in. I’ll sit there wiping my face and trying to look normal because I don’t want anyone asking what’s wrong. I don’t even always know what to say is wrong. It just feels like I’m carrying something really heavy around, and I’m tired of pretending I’m not.\n\nDoctor: Have you been enjoying things you usually like a lot less than usual?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure. I mean… maybe a little, but I don’t know how much is “a lot less than usual.”\n\nLike with work, I still like the actual materials—the stones, the metal, getting something to sit just right, that tiny satisfying click when a setting works. That part of me is still there, I think. But I guess I haven’t been getting the same kind of lift from it lately. Sometimes I’m doing it and I know I should feel pleased, like, “That turned out nice,” but it just sort of lands flat.\n\nBut then I also don’t know if that counts as not enjoying things or if I’m just tired and in my head. I haven’t really been keeping track. It’s not like I woke up one day and hated everything I used to like. It’s more subtle than that, I guess. Things just feel… dulled down a bit.\n\nDoctor: For at least the past two weeks, have you felt down or depressed most of the day nearly every day?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I mean, “most of the day” is hard because there are moments where I’m busy enough that I’m not actively thinking about it, especially if I’m focused on some tiny detail at the bench. But underneath it, it’s been there pretty much every day these past couple weeks.\n\nI wake up with it already sitting on me, like this heavy, hollow feeling, before anything has even happened. It’s like the shine has gone out of everything a little. And I’ve had mornings where I’m in my truck before opening the shop and I just start crying, and then I have to sit there and get myself together so I can go in and act normal. So… yeah, I’d say it’s been most days, and a lot of the day.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed your appetite being lower than usual lately?\n\nPatient: I don’t really know. I haven’t been paying close attention to it, honestly.\n\nSome days I eat because it’s just time to eat, not because I’m excited about food, but I’m not sure if that means my appetite is actually lower or if I’m just distracted and kind of going through the motions. Like, I’ll realize I haven’t thought about lunch, but then if food is there I can usually eat it. So I wouldn’t say I’ve clearly noticed a big drop or anything.\n\nIt’s more that meals feel like one more thing I’m supposed to do, if that makes sense. But no, I don’t have a definite answer like, “Yes, I’m barely eating.” I’m not sure.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling unusually tired or low on energy lately?\n\nPatient: Yeah. Definitely. That’s actually been one of the more obvious things, I think.\n\nIt’s not just normal tired, like “I had a long day at work.” It’s more like my body doesn’t want to cooperate. When I’m sitting at the bench, my arms feel like they’re made of lead sometimes. Even doing something I’ve done a thousand times—polishing a setting, holding steady for detail work, moving trays around—it feels like it takes way more out of me than it should.\n\nAnd carrying a tray of stones or getting things set up, stuff that normally wouldn’t even register as effort, can wipe me out. I’ll think, “Why am I this tired from that?” It’s embarrassing, honestly, because my work isn’t easy, but I know what normal work-tired feels like, and this feels different.\n\nEven if I get a full night off, it doesn’t really reset me. I can rest, or not do much, and then I’m still dragging the next day. It’s like the tiredness is already there waiting for me before I even start.\n\nDoctor: Lately, have you been feeling worthless or blaming yourself a lot?\n\nPatient: Yeah… yeah, I have. Probably more than I want to admit.\n\nIt’s like my brain keeps pulling up every client I promised something to and then didn’t follow through the way I should’ve. Even if it was fixable, even if nobody yelled at me, I keep replaying it like, “You told them you could handle this, and then you made yourself look stupid.” Or I’ll think about little mistakes at the shop and it turns into this whole thing where I’m not just a person who made a mistake, I’m a fraud. Like I’ve been pretending I’m better at this than I really am.\n\nAnd the family part… that’s been eating at me too. I had this award, and everyone made such a big deal out of it, like it meant I was really going somewhere. And now I feel like I embarrassed them by not living up to it. Like they were proud over this one thing, and I let them believe it meant something bigger than it did.\n\nI know that probably sounds dramatic when I say it out loud, but it feels very real in my head. I’ll sit there and go over it again and again, and it’s hard to talk myself out of it.\n\nDoctor: Have you had thoughts that you would be better off dead, or thoughts of hurting yourself?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I have. I don’t really like saying that out loud, but yes.\n\nIt’s not like I have some plan or that I’ve sat down and decided I’m going to do something to myself. I haven’t. But the thought keeps coming up that maybe people would be better off if I just wasn’t around. Like my family wouldn’t have to keep being disappointed or worried, and my clients wouldn’t be relying on me and getting let down. It’s this awful loop where I think, “Everyone’s life would probably be easier without me in it.”\n\nAnd then I get scared because it’s not just a random thought once and then it goes away. It comes back more often than I’m comfortable with. I’ll be trying to do something normal, or I’ll be alone for a minute, and there it is again. I don’t want to feel that way, and part of me knows it’s probably not a fair way to look at things, but in the moment it feels really convincing.\n\nSo… yes, I’ve had thoughts like that. Not a plan, but the thoughts are there, and they scare me.\n\nDoctor: Lately, have you been moving or talking noticeably slower than usual?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure, honestly. I haven’t noticed myself moving or talking slower in a way where I could clearly say, “Yes, that’s happening.”\n\nI mean, I’ve felt slowed down inside sometimes, if that makes sense, but I don’t know if other people would actually see it. No one’s said, like, “Why are you talking so slow?” or anything like that. At work I might take longer to get myself started or kind of sit there before doing the next step, but I don’t know if that’s me moving slower exactly, or just… I don’t know, taking longer to get going.\n\nWith talking, I don’t think so. I might pause more because I’m trying to find what I mean, but I’m not aware of my speech being noticeably slower. So I guess my answer is: not that I’ve clearly noticed, no.\n\nDoctor: Over the past two weeks, have you had much less interest or pleasure in things you usually enjoy?\n\nPatient: I’m honestly not sure how to answer that one cleanly.\n\nI mean, I know what you’re asking, like whether stuff I normally enjoy feels flat or pointless, but I haven’t really been keeping track of it in that way. If you asked me to list what I usually enjoy and then compare it to the last two weeks, I’d probably have to sit and think about each thing separately.\n\nI can say I haven’t had this very obvious moment of, “Oh, I don’t enjoy anything anymore.” It’s more confusing than that. Sometimes I’ll start doing something and I’m not sure if I’m enjoying it, or if I’m just doing it because it’s familiar or because it’s what I normally do. But I don’t know if that counts as “much less interest or pleasure,” or if I’m just overthinking the question.\n\nSo… I guess I’d say I’m not totally sure. I haven’t clearly noticed a major drop, but I also don’t feel like I can give you a confident yes or no.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },

    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah… that was actually one of the main things that made me think I needed help, even before I was thinking of it as being “depressed” or whatever.

At work especially, at the bench, my attention has been a real problem. I’ll be trying to work on a piece and my mind just will not stay with one thing. Like I’ll look at a customer email, then notice a saw mark I need to clean up, then hear some noise outside or someone moving around, and suddenly I’m off on that. Then I come back and realize I’ve kind of lost the thread of the whole work order — like where I was, what I had already checked, what still needed doing.

And with jewelry work, that’s not just annoying, you know? You have to keep track of tiny steps and details, and if I’m bouncing around like that, it creates real trouble. It’s not just me being a little distracted like anyone gets. It’s more like my mind keeps grabbing onto the next thing before I’ve finished the thing in front of me.

So yes, before these last couple weeks, the attention stuff was already causing clear problems in my work and daily functioning. That’s honestly a big part of why I came in.

I haven’t clearly noticed a major drop, but I also don’t feel like I can give you a confident yes or no.\n\nDoctor: Before these past two weeks of feeling depressed, were your attention problems already causing clear trouble at work or in daily life?\n\nPatient: Yeah… that was actually one of the main things that made me think I needed help, even before I was thinking of it as being “depressed” or whatever.\n\nAt work especially, at the bench, my attention has been a real problem. I’ll be trying to work on a piece and my mind just will not stay with one thing. Like I’ll look at a customer email, then notice a saw mark I need to clean up, then hear some noise outside or someone moving around, and suddenly I’m off on that. Then I come back and realize I’ve kind of lost the thread of the whole work order — like where I was, what I had already checked, what still needed doing.\n\nAnd with jewelry work, that’s not just annoying, you know? You have to keep track of tiny steps and details, and if I’m bouncing around like that, it creates real trouble. It’s not just me being a little distracted like anyone gets. It’s more like my mind keeps grabbing onto the next thing before I’ve finished the thing in front of me.\n\nSo yes, before these last couple weeks, the attention stuff was already causing clear problems in my work and daily functioning. Like, I’ll realize I haven’t thought about lunch, but then if food is there I can usually eat it. I haven’t clearly noticed a major drop, but I also don’t feel like I can give you a confident yes or no.\n\nDoctor: Before these past two weeks of feeling depressed, were your attention problems already causing clear trouble at work or in daily life?\n\nPatient: Yeah… that was actually one of the main things that made me think I needed help, even before I was thinking of it as being “depressed” or whatever.\n\nAt work especially, at the bench, my attention has been a real problem. I’ll be trying to work on a piece and my mind just will not stay with one thing. Like I’ll look at a customer email, then notice a saw mark I need to clean up, then hear some noise outside or someone moving around, and suddenly I’m off on that. Then I come back and realize I’ve kind of lost the thread of the whole work order — like where I was, what I had already checked, what still needed doing.\n\nAnd with jewelry work, that’s not just annoying, you know? You have to keep track of tiny steps and details, and if I’m bouncing around like that, it creates real trouble. It’s not just me being a little distracted like anyone gets. It’s more like my mind keeps grabbing onto the next thing before I’ve finished the thing in front of me.\n\nSo yes, before these last couple weeks, the attention stuff was already causing clear problems in my work and daily functioning. That’s honestly a big part of why I came in.\n\n[Last Question]\nBefore age 12, were you often having trouble paying attention or staying organized at school or at home?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Male / 27 / jeweler_or_precious_stone_or_metal_worker / high_school\n- severity: Moderate\n\n[Targeted Info]\nNone\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Verbose patient.\nA verbose patient may\n1) provide excessively detailed responses when describing their symptoms and experiences, even when the details are directly relevant,\n2) elaborate extensively on the background, events, thoughts, feelings, and personal experiences surrounding their symptoms, and\n3) demonstrate difficulty allowing the doctor to guide the interview or move on to another clinical topic.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I’m not really sure, honestly. That’s kind of hard for me to answer because I don’t remember that age super clearly in a way where I can say, “Yes, before 12 this was definitely a pattern.”

I mean, I remember school in a general sense, and I know I wasn’t some perfectly organized kid with color-coded everything, but I don’t know if that was abnormal or just being a kid. I’d have to think back to specific grades or maybe ask someone who knew me then, because my memory of it is kind of patchy.

At home too, I don’t really know. I’m sure there were times I lost things or didn’t keep my room organized, but I can’t confidently say it was often enough or serious enough to count as a problem before age 12.

So I guess my answer is: I don’t know. I can’t give you a clear yes from my own memory.
